## Page 1


Non-Signaling Locality Lower Bounds for Dominating Set
Noah Fleming*
Lund & Columbia
noah.fleming@cs.lth.se
Max Hopkins†
IAS
nmhopkin@ias.edu
Yuichi Yoshida‡
National Institute of Informatics
yyoshida@nii.ac.jp
April 6, 2026
Abstract
Minimum dominating set is a basic local covering problem and a core task in distributed comput-
ing. Despite extensive study, in the classic LOCAL model there exist significant gaps between known
algorithms and lower bounds. Chang and Li (PODC ’23) prove an Ω(log n)-locality lower bound for
a constant factor approximation, while Kuhn–Moscibroda–Wattenhofer (JACM ’16) provide an algo-
rithm beating this bound beyond log ∆-approximation, along with a weaker lower bound for this degree-
dependent setting scaling roughly with min{log ∆/ log log ∆,
p
log n/ log log n}. Unfortunately, this
latter bound is very weak for small ∆, and never recovers the Chang–Li bound, leaving central questions:
does O(log ∆)-approximation require Ω(log n) locality, and do such bounds extend beyond LOCAL?
In this work, we take a major step toward answering these questions in the non-signaling model,
which strictly subsumes the LOCAL, quantum-LOCAL, and bounded-dependence settings. We prove
every O(log ∆)-approximate non-signaling distribution for dominating set requires locality Ω(log n/(log ∆·
poly log log ∆)). Further, we show for some β ∈(0, 1), every O(logβ ∆)-approximate non-signaling
distribution requires locality Ω(log n/ log ∆), which combined with the KMW bound yields a degree-
independent Ω(
p
log n/ log log n) quantum-LOCAL lower bound for O(logβ ∆)-approximation algo-
rithms, removing the weak degree-dependent term from KMW’s classic result.
The proof is based on two new low-soundness sensitivity lower bounds for label cover, one via
Impagliazzo–Kabanets–Wigderson-style parallel repetition (SICOMP ’12) with degree reduction and
one from a sensitivity-preserving reworking of the Dinur–Harsha framework (SICOMP ’13), together
with the reductions from label cover to set cover to dominating set and the sensitivity-to-locality transfer
theorem of Fleming and Yoshida (SODA ’26).

Introduction
Minimum dominating set is a classic local covering problem asking for the smallest set of vertices in a graph
such that every vertex is either selected, or has a selected neighbor. The problem is a core optimization task in
distributed computing, especially in the LOCAL model, where the trade-off between approximation quality
and locality has been studied extensively [10, 13, 23, 30, 40, 42, 43], yet remains far from fully understood.
State of the art bounds for dominating set in the LOCAL model come in two flavors. On the one hand,
Chang and Li [10] show a strong Ω(log n) locality lower bound against some constant-factor approximation.
On the other hand, allowing a degree-dependent approximation factor, in seminal work Kuhn–Moscibroda–
Wattenhofer [42] prove this lower bound can be circumvented, achieving a (1 + ε) log ∆-approximation
with locality O(
log n
log(1+ε)) for any ε > 0, and complement this with a corresponding degree-dependent lower
bound showing any log ∆-approximation requires locality at least
Ω

.
Unfortunately, especially for smaller ∆, this latter bound is extremely weak, scaling just with log ∆, and
never achieves the Ω(log n) scale of Chang and Li. This has left open two central problems:
1. Can Chang and Li’s Ω(log n) lower bound be extended to O(log ∆)-approximation to match existing
algorithms?
2. Do such bounds extend beyond the LOCAL model to modern distributed settings such as quantum-
LOCAL and bounded-dependence? [2, 12, 29, 34]
In particular, while the KMW bound is known to extend to quantum-LOCAL and beyond [8], Chang and
Li’s is not, leaving an Ω(log n) bound open even for constant factor approximation.
In this work, we take a major step toward resolving these problems. We prove two new lower bounds
for minimum dominating set in the non-signaling model, where an algorithm has locality t if its output
distribution has the property that the marginals on any vertex set depend only on the radius-t neighborhood
of that set; this model strictly subsumes the LOCAL, quantum-LOCAL, and bounded-dependence models
at the same locality [2, 12, 29, 34, 45]. Our first main result extends Chang and Li’s Ω(log n) lower bound
all the way to O(log ∆)-approximation against non-signaling, albeit with locality that decays with larger ∆:
Theorem 1.1. There exists an integer ∆0 ≥2 and an absolute constant C > 0 such that for all sufficiently
large n and every integer ∆≥∆0, any C log ∆-approximate non-signaling distribution for the dominating
set problem on n-vertex graphs with maximum degree at most ∆requires locality at least
Ω

Since Theorem 1.1 is proved for non-signaling, it also applies to LOCAL. Thus for every fixed constant
∆with ∆≥∆0, we get an Ω(log n) lower bound matching the scale of Chang–Li while upgrading the ap-
proximation factor from some fixed constant to O(log ∆), essentially matching KMW’s LOCAL algorithm.
Theorem 1.1 is weaker for large ∆, but can be improved in combination with KMW’s bound to achieve
a degree-unconditional lower bound of ˜Ω(√log n) against O(log ∆)-approximation in quantum-LOCAL.
In fact, if one could remove the poly log log ∆term from Theorem 1.1, the KMW bound becomes stronger

1.1
Proof Overview
Transfer principle.
Our lower bounds are proved via a reduction to algorithmic sensitivity, which mea-
sures how much an algorithm’s output distribution can change after a local perturbation of the input, e.g., a
single changed constraint in a constraint satisfaction problem (CSP). In particular, we use a sensitivity-to-
locality transfer principle from [28] which converts sensitivity lower bounds for graph problems into lower
bounds for non-signaling algorithms. This transfer principle is simply the observation that, for any graph
problem on graphs of degree at most ∆, any non-signaling algorithm with locality t, when run on two in-
put instances perturbed in one location, must have the same output outside a radius-t ball surrounding the
change. This immediately implies any such algorithm has sensitivity at most ∆t, so if one can show that no,
say, poly(n)-sensitive algorithm exists for graphs of size n and degree ∆, then this implies a lower bound
against any locality t = log∆(n) non-signaling algorithm.
We defer the formal definitions of sensitivity to the preliminaries and here only overview our methods.
Reduction chain.
Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 1.2 are proved via the following chain of reductions:

Theorem 1.1
Theorem 13.3
The first arrow is the classical reduction from label cover (see Section 1.1.1 for an overview of the label cover
problem) to set cover, the second is the standard reduction from set cover to dominating set, and the last is the
sensitivity-to-locality transfer theorem. Together they yield set-cover and dominating-set sensitivity lower
bounds and, through the transfer principle of [28], the locality theorems above. For the Dinur–Harsha route,
these steps are packaged as the black-box chain shown in the lower row. For the IKW route, the upper row
is now the direct chain
Theorem 1.5 →Theorem 12.4 →Theorem 13.3 →Theorem 1.1.
Thus Theorem 1.5 supplies the low-soundness label-cover hardness, Theorem 12.4 turns it into a set-cover
lower bound, Theorem 13.3 lifts this to bounded-degree dominating set, and the transfer principle yields
Theorem 1.1.
Key Ingredients.
Existing label cover sensitivity lower bounds [28] only handle the high-soundness
regime, roughly a (1 −ε)-approximation on satisfiable instances for a fixed constant ε > 0. This is far
too weak for the reductions to set cover and dominating set, which require low-soundness label cover in-
stances of the kind used in classical PCP hardness reductions [27, 33]. Unfortunately, low soundness PCPs
are famously harder to construct and analyze than their high soundness counterpart. The following two
theorems, our main technical contribution, supply sensitivity lower bounds for such label cover instances,
which we use to prove Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 1.2 respectively.
Before stating the results, we briefly note that both results below are stated for left-predicate label cover.
Definition 1.4 (Label Cover). Recall that a 2-CSP consists of an underlying graph G = (V, E), an alphabet
{Σv} of possible values for each vertex, and, for each edge e = (v, v′), a constraint fe defining a set of
satisfying assignments in Σv × Σv′. The value of the instance is the maximum over assignments π to V
3


## Page 7


of the total fraction of satisfied constraints. A label cover instance is a special 2-CSP consisting of a tuple
(U, V, E, ΣU, ΣV , F) where G = (U, V, E) is a bipartite graph, ΣU is a fixed alphabet for the left-hand
side, ΣV is a fixed alphabet for the right-hand side, and F = {fe : ΣU →ΣV } (the ‘edge projections’)
determines the constraints by requiring π(v) = fe(π(u)) for any edge e = (u, v).
The left-predicate label cover problem is a slight generalization of the above in which each left vertex
u carries a unary predicate; if this predicate evaluates to 0, then all projection constraints incident to u are
violated. Ordinary label cover is recovered by taking all left predicates to be identically 1. We use this
language because, in the reductions used later, a local change in the instance before the reduction can be
encoded by changing only the left-side predicates in the resulting label cover instance, while keeping the
underlying graph and edge projections fixed; this is exactly the form needed for the sensitivity argument.
For this overview, we suggest that the reader think of this simply as label cover.
Theorem 1.5. There exists C > 0 such that for all sufficiently large N ∈N and k ≤o(log N), any algo-
rithm that, on satisfiable left-predicate label cover instances on n = NΘ(k) vertices, outputs an exp (−C
√
k)-
satisfying solution must have sensitivity at least nΩ(1/k). Moreover, the hard instances can be taken to be
biregular with alphabet and degree parameters at most 2O(k).
Roughly speaking, to prove lower bounds for max-degree ∆dominating set, one should think of setting
k = poly log log ∆above, resulting in an n1/ poly log log ∆-sensitivity lower bound. Traced through the
reduction, the exponent becomes the additional poly log log ∆-term in Theorem 1.1 that we’d like to remove.
With this in mind, we give the following alternate truly polynomial sensitivity lower bound:
Theorem 1.6. There exist absolute constants B, δ > 0 such that for every function 1 < g(n) ≤(log n)B
and all sufficiently large n, any algorithm that, given a satisfiable left-predicate label cover instance with
n = |U|-many left vertices, outputs an assignment that satisfies at least a 1/g(n) fraction of the constraints
must have sensitivity at least Ω(nδ). Moreover, the hard instances can be chosen so that |U| = n, |V | =
n · (log n)O(1), the left and right degrees are g(n)O(1), |ΣV | = g(n)O(1), and |ΣU| ≤exp
 g(n)O(1)
.
Unfortunately, Theorem 1.6 cannot be used to prove a lower bound against O(log ∆)-approximation for
dominating set due to the large left-alphabet size, which restricts us to O(logβ ∆)-approximation for some
β > 0. More generally, the degree ∆of the final dominating set instance in our reduction is at least the
degree and alphabet size of the starting low soundness label cover, so it is critical we keep these parameters
as small as possible. Improving the alphabet-size bound in the PCP underlying Theorem 1.6 is a central
open problem in PCP theory, related to the so-called ‘sliding scale conjecture’, and doing so would have
consequences far beyond just improving our dominating set lower bound [48].
Below, we give an overview of the proofs of Theorem 1.5 and Theorem 1.6. Before this, we describe
the challenges that go into developing a PCP theorem that preserves sensitivity.
The Challenge of Maintaining Sensitivity.
Classical PCP theorems for hardness of approximation track
how the completeness, soundness, and structural parameters of a label cover instance change as it proceeds
through the PCP. For sensitivity, this is not enough. Because sensitivity compares the behavior of an al-
gorithm on two nearby source instances rather than a single instance in isolation, we need to ensure the
reduction remains stable applied to instances related by any one local modification, which we call a source
swap. In other words, instead of working with single instance, formally we will have to work with and
control the behavior of an entire swap-closed family1 of instances throughout the reduction.
1That is, once an instance is in the family, every instance obtained from it by one source swap also remains in the family; see
Section 2 for the formal definition.
4


## Page 8


Recall our goal is really to transfer a sensitivity lower bound backward through the PCP reduction, which
we call sensitivity pullback. For this, each stage of the PCP reduction must come with a recovery map that
converts a labeling of the transformed instance Ii+1 into a labeling of the source instance Ii. Once such a
recovery map is available, there are three effects of a source swap that must be controlled:
−how much the transformed instance itself can change;
−how much the recovery map amplifies a perturbation of a labeling on the transformed instance; and
−how much the recovery map itself changes when the source instance changes.
This is a substantially stronger requirement than the usual completeness/soundness bookkeeping required by
an ordinary PCP theorem. To make it possible, we design our reductions so that all instances in the family
live on a common underlying graph, and a source swap changes only local predicates, not the graph or the
edge projections.
1.1.1
Proof Overview of Theorem 1.5
The proof of Theorem 1.5 consists of two main steps: 1) a new “sensitivity-preserving” analysis and recovery
map for (combinatorial) parallel repetition [38], which amplifies soundness (approximation factor) of a base
label-cover instance at the cost of massively blowing up its size and (right)-degree, and 2) a new “sensitivity-
amplifying” degree-reduction procedure that simultaneously reduces this right-degree while improving the
right-sensitivity.2 We overview these two steps below along with some corresponding background.
Combinatorial Parallel Repetition.
The low-soundness combinatorial parallel repetition theorem of Im-
pagliazzo, Kabanets, and Wigderson (IKW) [38] amplifies a base 2-CSP Φ with alphabet Σ on underlying
graph GΦ = (V, E) to a label-cover instance Φ′ = (U′, V ′, E′, Σ′

We would like to show that if the base-CSP Φ on N vertices has no (say) N1/2-sensitive algorithm that
outputs a (1 −δ)-satisfying solution on satisfiable inputs, then Φ′ likewise has no N1/4 = nΩ(1/k)-sensitive

Imbalanced Graphs and Sensitivity.
Unfortunately, we will not be able to show such a strong sensitivity
lower bound directly due to the fact that the label-cover instance described above is extremely imbalanced.
To illustrate the problem, let us first outline what the above reduction should look like.
As described at a high level above, our basic approach is to start with neighboring instances I and ˜I of
the original 2-CSP that differ in one constraint, and map both instances to their corresponding IKW label
cover instances. To prove a lower bound against T-sensitive algorithms for the resulting label cover, we’ll
assume toward contradiction we in fact have such an algorithm. Our goal is to run this algorithm on the
embeddings of I and ˜I to produce solutions π and ˜π, then give a ‘recovery map’ that maps these solutions
back to highly satisfying solutions to the base CSP R(π) and R(˜π) whose Hamming distance is ≪
√
N in
expectation, violating the sensitivity lower bound for the base CSP.
What actually happens when we run this approach? Well, if I and ˜I differ by one constraint, the corre-
sponding label cover instances differ in roughly a Θ(k/N) fraction of constraints, the probability we sample
a modified constraint in steps (1-3) above. Running a T-sensitive algorithm on these embedded neighbor-
ing instances would therefore produce solutions π and ˜π that differ in up to T|E′| k
N locations. This is a
problem: due to the imbalance of the graph, even if T = 1, T|E′| k
N ≫|V ′|. In other words, the entire
right-hand side of the two embedded label cover instances could be completely different!
We’ll handle this by proving a weaker lower bound which separately controls the sensitivity on the left
and right parts of the label-cover instance. In particular, we will show there is no algorithm which is N1/4-
sensitive on the left-hand side, and |E′|
|V ′|N1/4-sensitive on the right-hand side, normalizing for this degree
imbalance. Note that this latter quantity is much less than 1, which seems like an extremely weak lower
bound. Nevertheless, we will see this guarantee, which controls how much the right-hand side changes in
expectation, is in fact still strong enough to achieve the final desired poly(N)-bound after degree reduction,
which roughly amplifies this term by a corresponding |V ′|
|E′| factor.

inspired by similar algorithms in replicable learning [39]:
1. Repeat the recovery procedure several times independently
2. Pick a random t ∈[1/2, 1]
3. Output the first solution with value at least 1 −tδ
Repeating the procedure independently ensures at least one good output with high probability. Since the
recovered solutions from π and ˜π are very close, their values will be close, and as a result the randomized
thresholding procedure in Steps 2/3 will almost certainly select close solutions to the base CSP as desired.
It remains to argue the initial recovery procedure indeed produces a high value solution to the base
CSP. This is the most technical part of the argument, and is deferred to Section 5. The high level idea is
to show that the decoding procedure is typically close to the majority decoding value of x conditioned on
πV ′(A) = a. This majority-based decoding is already known to have high value due to the PCP reduction
of [38], but may have poor sensitivity. The proof of closeness to majority follows via concentration-of-
measure properties of the graph underlying the IKW label-cover, which control the probability that the label
of a random x ∈V is far from its expectation, even after conditioning on various events like πV ′(A) = a.
Degree Reduction
The produced label-cover instance above has poor right-sensitivity and corresponding
high right degree; the second key step is to give a degree-reduction procedure rectifying this. Toward this
end, we use a procedure from Dinur and Harsha [20] that reduces the right degree to exp(O(k′)) (polynomial
in the soundness), and, critically, give a recovery map which actually amplifies the corresponding right-
sensitivity lower bound by a factor of |E′|
|V ′|, the original right-degree. Plugging this into the sensitivity bound
above gives the desired poly(N)-sensitivity lower bound for low-soundness label cover of Theorem 1.5.
In slightly more detail, degree-reduction proceeds by replacing each right vertex v, with degree Dv, by a
cloud of Dv-many vertices. An expander is then used to wire these new vertices to the old neighborhood of
v. If u was a neighbor of v and it is now a neighbor of a vertex v′ in the cloud, then the edge (u, v′) inherits
the constraint of (u, v). The expander ensures that the constraints of v’s edges are dispersed uniformly
among the cloud, making them a good approximation to the original constraint-set. Critically this introduces
some extra redundancy: the assignment for v is duplicated among the vertices in its cloud. We design a
recovery procedure which takes advantage of this redundancy to increase the sensitivity lower bound by a
corresponding factor in the size of the cloud (the original right-degree) as desired.
1.1.2
Proof Overview of Theorem 1.6
The proof of Theorem 1.6 has two main steps. First, we construct an initial label-cover family, which we call
the stage-0 construction, with very small soundness but very large alphabet, degree, and local description
complexity, an additional measure needed for composition roughly capturing the complexity of computing
whether a given assignment to the neighborhood of some left vertex u has a potential satisfying label for u.
Second, we apply a Dinur–Harsha style iterated composition theorem [20] to move to the target soundness
scale 1/g(n) while reducing these structural parameters to the corresponding scale.
As we adapt the Dinur–Harsha framework, we recall only the part relevant below. Stage-0 already gives
a soundness threshold much smaller than the one needed in the final theorem, but its alphabet size, degrees,
and local description complexity are tuned to that overly strong regime. The iteration weakens the soundness
guarantee in a controlled way and, at the same time, reshapes the local structure so that these parameters can
be reduced to the new scale.
7


## Page 11


Dinur–Harsha Composition.
A label cover instance asks, at each left vertex u, whether the labels on
the neighboring right vertices form an admissible tuple, namely one that can be extended to some left label
satisfying the incident projections and the left predicate. Composition replaces this admissibility check by
a local proof-verification task: one supplies a short auxiliary proof together with a local decoder that probes
only a few proof positions and either recovers the relevant component of an admissible tuple or rejects at the
cost of increasing the soundness threshold from εi to the weaker scale εi+1.
After composition, the resulting instance has a simpler local certification, but its structural parameters
are still not in the form needed for the next round. The cleanup steps—alphabet reduction and degree
reduction—convert this composed instance back into a left-predicate label cover whose degree, alphabet,
and local-description parameters are polynomial in 1/εi+1. Thus, one stage has the form
Ii
compose
−−−−→Ji+1
alphabet/degree reduction
−−−−−−−−−−−−−→Ii+1.
The intermediate instance Ji+1 exposes the proof-based certification created by composition, while the
cleanup step restores the interface needed for the next stage. Repeating these two steps moves the con-
struction from the very small soundness of stage-0 to the target scale 1/g(n) while keeping the structural
parameters under control. The rest of the overview explains how we stabilize this template for sensitivity.
Stage-0: Keeping Clause-Swaps Local.
The first obstacle already appears in the initialization I0 of the
iteration. The hard family that we ultimately care about starts as bounded-degree satisfiable 2-CSP in-
stances (we take the instance constructed in the lower bound proof of Fleming and Yoshida [28]), but before
the Dinur–Harsha iteration begins, we must first convert that family—via a constant-size gadget—into a
bounded-degree satisfiable CNF family; the instance I0 for stage-0 is the robust algebraic encoding of this
CNF family into a left-predicate label cover instance. In the standard Dinur–Harsha template, the formula
is represented by its clause-indicator table. It is then extended to a global low-degree object, and then
checked by the usual algebraic consistency tests, which verify its degree and that it does indeed represent
the clause-indicator table. This is excellent for classical soundness, because the low-degree structure gives
strong global control. For sensitivity, however, it creates exactly the wrong kind of dependence on the source
formula: swapping one clause for another (a clause swap) changes one entry of the clause table before alge-
braization, but after low-degree extension the encoded object typically changes at many points, so one local
clause swap becomes a global change in the label-cover instance.
Our new stage-0 construction breaks this dependence into two separate tasks. The outer algebraic layer
certifies only the low-degree structure of the assignment and auxiliary witness polynomials. The question
“what is the value of the clause table at this particular location?” is delegated to a small Reed–Muller
decode/reject gadget that either returns the requested table value or rejects. Because the CNF now enters only
through this local lookup, the formula-dependent part of the construction is confined to the left predicates.
Consequently, one clause swap changes only a controlled number of left predicates, while the underlying
graph and edge projections remain fixed. This yields the left-predicate label cover under clause swaps that
serves as the stage-0 starting family for the rest of the argument.
Finally, stage-0 must also provide a stable way to recover a satisfying assignment to the initial CNF
formula for sensitivity pullback. Given an assignment to the stage-0 label cover instance, we extract a tuple
of candidate low-degree polynomials which claim to encode the low-degree extension (as well as some
auxiliary witnesses), and score them by the fraction of local checks they explain. We then select one of the
high-scoring candidates using a random threshold. This produces a satisfying assignment with noticeable
probability, in a form that remains stable under local perturbations for the later pullback argument.
8


## Page 12


Stable Composition: Padding the Graph.
Next, we modify the composition procedure from Dinur-
Harsha [20] to preserve sensitivity. After composition, the constraints on the edges incident to a left vertex
u check whether the joint labeling of the right vertices that are adjacent to u are compatible with u’s label
and all incident projections. The inner decoder is asked to certify exactly this local compatibility condition,
rather than to reason about the full left alphabet directly.
In analyzing sensitivity, this standard approach once again hits a barrier: in a naive composed instance,
the decoder’s chosen proof locations determine which new edges appear in the composed graph. A local
source change can then alter which locations are queried, and hence alter the graph of the composed instance
itself. To prevent this, we implement the composition on a single padded graph shared by all instances in
the family. Each composed left label is viewed as a fixed-size matrix whose rows correspond to the potential
neighboring constraints of the underlying outer right vertex and whose columns correspond to positions in
the inner decoder’s proof string. The rows corresponding to actual neighbors are active, and the remaining
rows are dummy padding. All coordinate-projection edges from each active row to every column are placed
in advance. The decoder’s actual queries are then used only inside the left predicate rather than being
encoded by the edge set. With this padding, a source swap may change which rows and columns are
semantically relevant, but it does not change the underlying graph.
Stable Cleanup: Freezing the Auxiliary Choices.
After composition, one still has to repair the local
parameters before the next round. This is the role of the alphabet-then-degree reduction. In a classical
hardness proof these gadgets are used only as parameter-improving transformations, so one is free to choose
whatever code or expander is convenient at that moment. In the sensitivity setting, that freedom is dangerous:
if nearby source instances were sent through different auxiliary choices, then the reduction itself could vary
globally even when the source change was local.
We therefore freeze these auxiliary choices on each swap-closed family. In particular, the code used in
alphabet reduction and the expanders used in the subsequent degree-reduction step are fixed once-and-for-
all on that family. This matters because the alphabet-reduction recovery samples each recovered right label
from the empirical distribution induced by the incident transformed edges at that vertex, and that distribution
depends not only on the transformed labeling but also on the source instance through the underlying pro-
jections. Hence “stability under perturbing a labeling on a fixed transformed instance” and “stability under
changing the source instance” are different statements. Freezing the auxiliary choices keeps the reduction
itself fixed, so that only the predicates and the resulting recovery maps vary across the family.
How Iterations Fit Together.
With these modifications in place, the rest of the proof can be read as a sta-
bilized version of Dinur–Harsha iteration. We start from the stage-0 family described above, and that stage-0
construction begins with a smaller soundness threshold than the one ultimately required in the theorem. At
stage i, composition weakens the soundness guarantee from εi to εi+1 = 20ε1/4
i
. The cleanup step then
restores the degree, alphabet, and local-description bounds needed to run the next round at the new scale.
As the starting point for the iteration, we use the high-soundness hard family of Fleming and Yoshida [28],
namely a swap-closed family of satisfiable bounded-degree 2-CSP instances with constant alphabet and de-
gree. A constant-size gadget first converts this family into the bounded-degree satisfiable CNF family used
by the stage-0 construction above.
At stage i we maintain a family Ii on one common underlying graph, together with an instance map from
that base family and a recovery map back to it, and we track the three stability quantities above. The stage
parameter is denoted εi: smaller εi means stronger soundness, while the left and right degrees, alphabet
sizes, local description complexity, and vertex blow-up at stage i are polynomial in 1/εi. One stage has the

1.2
Further Related Work
Dominating Set, Label Cover, and the Non-Signaling Model
The non-signaling model (also known
as the ϕ-LOCAL or causal model) has emerged as the gold standard for distributed computing lower
bounds, automatically extending to the popular LOCAL, quantum-LOCAL, and bounded-dependence mod-
els [2, 4, 12, 29]. Fleming and Yoshida [28] first connected non-signaling locality to sensitivity, obtaining
non-signaling lower bounds for problems such as minimum vertex cover and maximum cut, but their meth-
ods (based on high-soundness label cover) do not extend directly to other classic distributed problems like
dominating set. For dominating set, Ω(log n) lower bounds were only known in the LOCAL model and
only for constant-factor approximation. In particular, Chang and Li [10] prove an Ω(log n) lower bound
for some constant approximation, while Kuhn, Moscibroda, and Wattenhofer [42] give a O(
log n
log(1+ε))-round
LOCAL algorithm for (1 + ε) log ∆-approximate dominating set along with a weaker degree-dependent
Ω(min{log ∆/ log log ∆,
p
log n/ log log n})-round lower bound against polylog(∆)-approximation. The
latter bound was also known to hold for quantum-LOCAL [8, 13, 42], but has remained open for the
bounded-dependence and non-signaling settings.
Reductions from low soundness label cover to set cover and dominating set are a classical approach
in hardness of approximation. Lund and Yannakakis [47] introduced the set-system framework underlying
hardness for minimization problems, Feige [27] established the threshold hardness of set cover via reduction
from label cover, and Chleb´ık and Chleb´ıkov´a [11] proved bounded-degree dominating-set hardness. These
are the reductions that underlie Sections 12 and 13; our use differs in that we reconstruct them in a form
compatible with the later sensitivity pullback, rather than using them only to transfer approximation gaps.
Sensitivity
The notion of sensitivity was introduced by Murai and Yoshida [50] to study the stability of
network centrality measures. Subsequent work focused largely on problem-specific sensitivity analyses and
low-sensitivity algorithms for broader graph problems [53, 55, 56]. The notion is also related to average
sensitivity [26, 31, 44, 54], where the average is taken over deleted elements, as well as to differential
privacy [24] and stability in learning [39]. We draw on some techniques from this literature.
On the lower-bound side, Fleming and Yoshida [28] established the first lower bounds on the sensitivity
of approximation algorithms for label cover, but only in the high-soundness regime. Since our non-signaling
lower bounds for dominating set proceed through the aforementioned classic reductions from label cover
to set cover and dominating set, the missing ingredient was analogous lower bounds in the delicate low-
soundness regime that are strong enough to survive those reductions; this paper supplies them.
Low Soundness PCPs
Low-soundness label cover is a central problem in PCP-based hardness of ap-
proximation [33]. The relevant constructions in this regime include the low-soundness two-query PCP of
Moshkovitz and Raz [49], the closely related PCP composition framework of Dinur and Harsha [20], par-
allel repetition and direct product testing [38, 52], and new derandomized direct product theorems [5, 6, 7,
10


## Page 14


15, 16, 17, 22, 51] based on high dimensional expanders. It is worth noting that latter give a promising ap-
proach towards a “best of both worlds” bound (i.e. combining the approximation factor of Theorem 1.1 and
locality bound of Theorem 1.2), in part due to a new local recovery algorithm for related instances [19], but
major barriers to this approach remain including 1) a ‘routing/embedding’ step in such reductions that ruins
sensitivity, and 2) the poor alphabet-soundness trade-off achieved by current HDX-based constructions.
We emphasize that the contribution of this work is not a new PCP theorem; it is a method for proving
sensitivity lower bounds that can exploit these often highly involved low-soundness label cover constructions
while retaining the parameters needed for downstream applications to set cover and dominating set.
1.3
Organization of the Paper
Sections 3 to 5 develop the Impagliazzo–Kabanets–Wigderson route to low-soundness label cover and prove
Theorem 1.5. Sections 6 to 11 develop the Dinur–Harsha route and prove Theorem 1.6. Sections 12 and 13
then organize the covering reductions by problem and prove Theorems 1.1 and 1.2.
2
Preliminaries
For a positive integer n, let [n] denote the set {1, 2, . . . , n}. We use bold symbols such as π to denote random
assignments, random labelings, and other random output objects produced by algorithms or recovery maps;
deterministic assignments and labelings are written without boldface, and symbols such as Π are reserved
for probability distributions and couplings.
2.1
Label Cover
We work with a slightly generalized label cover formalism. A label cover instance is a bipartite projection
game, and here we allow a unary left predicate on each left vertex. Ordinary label cover is recovered by
taking all of these predicates to be identically 1.
Definition 2.1 (Left-predicate label cover instance). A left-predicate label cover instance is a tuple
I = (U, V, E, ΣU, ΣV , P, F),
P = {Pu : ΣU →{0, 1}}u∈U,
F = {fe : ΣU →ΣV }e∈E,
where G = (U, V, E) is a bipartite graph, ΣU and ΣV are finite alphabets, each Pu is a unary predicate on
left labels, and each fe is a projection. An assignment is a pair π = (πU, πV ) with πU : U →ΣU and
πV : V →ΣV . An edge e = (u, v) is satisfied by π if
Pu(πU(u)) = 1
and
f(u,v)(πU(u)) = πV (v).
Thus a left label that fails its predicate satisfies none of its incident edges. For an assignment π, let

We define a variant of sensitivity that is more convenient when studying the label cover problem, called
swap sensitivity. Let I = (U, V, E, ΣU, ΣV , P, F) be a left-predicate label cover instance. For e ∈E and

u ∈U and a predicate Q : ΣU →{0, 1}, let Iu←Q denote the instance obtained from I by replacing Pu
with Q. Then, the swap sensitivity of a randomized algorithm A on I is defined as

Part I
Low-Soundness Label Cover via
Impagliazzo–Kabanets–Wigderson
In this chapter we prove Theorem 1.5, a sensitivity lower bound for low-soundness label cover based on the
combinatorial parallel repetition theorem of Impagliazzo, Kabanets, and Wigderson (IKW) [38]. The sec-
tion is split into three components. In Section 3, we introduce left/right sensitivity regions and the reduction
lemma that uses them to transfer sensitivity lower bounds through reductions. In Section 4, we analyze a
right-degree reduction procedure that reduces the right-degree of label cover while simultaneously amplify-
ing its right-sensitivity by a corresponding factor. Finally, in Section 5, we introduce the IKW construction
and a new corresponding low-sensitivity recovery procedure that combined with degree-reduction completes
the proof.
3
Left/Right Sensitivity Regions
In this section, we set up an abstract framework for transferring sensitivity lower bounds through reductions.
Concretely, we consider an instance transformation T together with a recovery map R that converts a label-
ing of the transformed instance back to a labeling of the source instance, and we identify conditions under
which such a pair lets us pull a sensitivity lower bound back to the source family.
To make this framework work in label cover, we need to track perturbations on the left and right sep-
arately, since a reduction may amplify them by different factors. If a label cover algorithm has sensitivity
k, then swapping a single constraint can alter at most kL labels on the left and kR labels on the right, with
13


## Page 17


kL +kR ≤k. It is therefore more informative to reason about the pair (kL, kR) than only their sum. For our
reduction arguments, we package this information as an upward-closed region of feasible left/right budget
vectors, which is the form that composes cleanly when couplings are chained along a swap path.
To formalize this idea, we record which expected left/right perturbation budgets are attainable under
couplings.

Definition 3.1 (Sensitivity-Region-Preserving Reduction). Let I be a family of ordinary label cover in-
stances or left-predicate label cover instances on the same underlying graph and domain. Let T be a pro-

4
Right-Degree Reduction
In this section we describe a procedure which converts a label cover instance to one in which every right-
vertex has the same low degree, while preserving any sensitivity lower bound that we have on the original
instance.
Definition 4.1 (Expanders). A d-regular graph G = (V, E) on n vertices is said to be an [n, d, λ]-expander
if every eigenvalue of its normalized adjacency matrix other than the top one is at most λ in absolute value
(equivalently, the second-largest absolute eigenvalue is at most λ).
Proposition 4.2 (Explicit Quasi-Ramanujan graphs, [3]). There exists a uniform algorithm that, for every
d > 3 and every sufficiently large n ∈N with nd even, constructs an [n, d, λ]-expander with λ = O(d−1/2)
in time polynomial in n and d.
Let d ≥4 be right-degree that we would like our label cover instance to have. Degree reduction proceeds
by replacing each right-vertex v, with degree Dv, by a “cloud” of Dv-many new vertices. We replace the
edges to v by edges from the Dv-many vertices to the neighborhood of v by using a d-regular expander graph
as a temple; this disperses the information stored at v among the Dv vertices. We describe this procedure,
which can be seen pictorially in Figure 1, formally next.
Because Proposition 4.2 only guarantees expanders for pairs (n, d) satisfying its hypotheses, we for-
mulate the degree-reduction map relative to a fixed choice of expanders. Fix an even integer d ≥4 and a
parameter λ ∈[0, 1). Let I = (U, V, E, ΣU, ΣV , P, F) be a left-predicate label cover instance whose right
degrees satisfy deg(v) ≥d for every v ∈V . For each right vertex v ∈V , let Dv denote its degree and
set δV := minv∈V Dv. Since d is even, the parity condition Dvd even from Proposition 4.2 is automatic
for every v ∈V . A (d, λ)-expander package for I is a choice, for each v ∈V , of a [Dv, d, λ]-expander
Hv = ([Dv], E′′). Once such a package is fixed, we define the transformation TDR(I; d) as follows. We fix
16


## Page 20


an arbitrary ordering of the neighbors of each right vertex v ∈V , and denote the i-th neighbor of v in the
bipartite graph G = (U, V, E) by ΓG(v, i). Let ΓHv(i, k) denote the k-th neighbor of i in Hv.
Algorithm 1: Right-Degree Reduction TDR
Input: A left-predicate label cover instance I = (U, V, E, ΣU, ΣV , P, F), an integer d ≥4, a
(d, λ)-expander package, and an ordering on the neighbors of each vertex
Output: An instance I′ = (U, V ′, E′, ΣU, ΣV , P, F ′) with right-degree d.

We observe some properties of the instance output by degree-reduction. First, |V ′| = |E|. For G′ =
(V ′, E′), each neighborhood satisfies ΓG′((v, i), k) := ΓG(v, ΓHv(i, k)). Finally, observe that the degree
of each vertex u ∈U in G′ becomes d · degG(u).

5
Low-Soundness Label Cover Route via Direct Product Testing
5.1
The IKW Construction and Its Label-Cover Consequence
We are now ready to prove the main sensitivity lower bound of this chapter, Theorem 1.5. We re-state the
result for convenience.
Theorem 5.1. There exists C > 0 such that for all sufficiently large NIKW ∈N and k ≤o(log NIKW),
any algorithm that, on satisfiable left-predicate label cover instances on n = NΘ(k)
IKW vertices, outputs an
exp (−C
√
k)-satisfying solution must have sensitivity at least nΩ(1/k). Moreover, the hard instances can be
taken to be biregular with alphabet and degree parameters at most 2O(k).
Theorem 5.1 is proved using a modification of the combinatorial parallel repetition (direct product)
theorem of Impagliazzo, Kabanets, and Wigderson (IKW) [38]. We note above and throughout this section,
we write NIKW for the size of the base 2CSP used by the direct-product construction, and n in the statement
for the final label cover size. We start by overviewing the construction and relevant parameters below.
Parameter guide.
Let NIKW denotes the number of variables in the base 2CSP. This separates the base-
instance size used by the IKW construction from the final label cover left-side size nLC used in later sections.
We first recall the label cover instance analyzed by IKW [38] in the perfect completeness regime. The
construction starts with a ‘slightly hard’ (constant soundness) base 2-CSP Φ on a d-regular graph G =
([NIKW], E) with alphabet Σ. We first describe the constraint graph of IKW’s amplified instance, which we

k . Fix k ∈N and some k′ = Θ(

1. Sample a size k′ subset A ⊂[NIKW] (i.e. a random vertex in V )
2. Sample a random edge for each vertex in A, and call the resulting edge-set AE,26 where AE,2 ⊆E
and |AE,2| = k′
3. Sample k −k′ additional random edges BE,2 ⊆E without replacement7 so that AE,2 ∩BE,2 = ∅.
4. Output the edge ({AE,2, BE,2}, A)
In particular, every sampled pair (AE,2, BE,2) determines a left vertex S = (AE,2, BE,2) ∈U, while the
right endpoint A lies in V =

original vertices in (AE,2, BE,2)), and the right alphabet is ΣV = Σk′ (one symbol per original vertex in A).
For (πU, πV ) to satisfy the edge ({AE,2, BE,2}, A) ∈EIKW, it must hold that
1. The left label πU(AE,2, BE,2) satisfies all constraints in BE,2 in the original CSP, and
2. πU’s vertex assignments on A agree with πV (A).
The left-predicates Pu enforce the first of these constraints, and the associated projection f{AE,2,BE,2},A ∈F
enforces the second. To align with our global notation, we denote the left labeling by πU and write ˜πU for the
corresponding labeling produced on a neighboring instance. Formally, πU : U →ΣU and ˜πU : U →ΣU,
and similarly πV : V →ΣV and ˜πV : V →ΣV .

5.2
Proof of the IKW Sensitivity Theorem
We briefly outline the proof of Theorem 5.2. We start with two neighboring instances IΦ and ˜IΦ of the
original CSP. We map both instances through the IKW encoding and show that the outputs remain reasonably
close. Assuming a low-sensitivity algorithm for the IKW CSP, we run it on the encodings of IΦ and ˜IΦ to
obtain two solutions to the IKW CSP that remain reasonably close. We then give a simple local decoding
algorithm for the IKW CSP that preserves sensitivity while outputting a highly satisfying solution to the
base CSP with non-trivial probability. Finally, we run this decoder sufficiently many times to ensure that we
obtain a highly satisfying solution with high probability, and apply a low-sensitivity selection rule to output
a good, low-sensitivity solution to the original CSP, contradicting the base CSP’s lower bound.
Proof of Theorem 5.2. We start with the first claim that the IKW encoding has relatively low (normalized)
sensitivity. In particular, given an instance IΦ, write IKWk(IΦ) to denote its encoded form. We claim:
Claim 5.3 (Encoding Blowup). Let IΦ, ˜IΦ be one-swap neighboring instances of a d-regular 2-CSP on
NIKW variables. The fraction of constraints differing between IKWk(IΦ) and IKWk(˜IΦ) is at most O(
k
dNIKW ).

Part II
Low-Soundness Label Cover via Dinur–Harsha
In this chapter we prove our sensitivity lower bound for low-soundness label cover based on the composition
theorem of Dinur and Harsha [20], which we restate next.
Theorem 1.6. There exist absolute constants B, δ > 0 such that for every function 1 < g(n) ≤(log n)B
and all sufficiently large n, any algorithm that, given a satisfiable left-predicate label cover instance with
n = |U|-many left vertices, outputs an assignment that satisfies at least a 1/g(n) fraction of the constraints
must have sensitivity at least Ω(nδ). Moreover, the hard instances can be chosen so that |U| = n, |V | =
n · (log n)O(1), the left and right degrees are g(n)O(1), |ΣV | = g(n)O(1), and |ΣU| ≤exp
 g(n)O(1)
.
This section is organized as follows. First, in Section 6 we introduce a notion of sensitivity-preserving
reduction similar to the one used in Section 5.2; the key differences are that we now only track sensitiv-
ity, rather than sensitive regions, and we allow the recovery map to depend on the source instance. In
Section 8, from the input family of CNF formulas, we construct the label cover instance that will be the
basis for the “stage-0” instance of our iterative construction of the final label cover instance that is done in
Section 11. Each stage of this iterative construction proceeds by the composition procedure adapted from
Dinur–Harsha [20], which we describe in Section 10, followed by a right-degree reduction which we de-
sign in Section 7, and an right-alphabet reduction procedure given in Section 9. The stage-0 instance is
constructed, these procedures are put together, and the final iterative construction is done in Section 11.
28


## Page 32


6
Sensitivity Reduction with Source-Dependent Recovery
In Part I we introduced a left/right budget-valued framework for transferring sensitivity lower bounds
through reductions. For the Dinur–Harsha route we only need its scalar analogue: we collapse the pair
(kL, kR) to total Hamming sensitivity, and we additionally allow the recovery map to depend on the source
instance. This contributes an additive term D when the source instance changes but the transformed assign-
ment is held fixed. The resulting scalar sensitivity-reduction interface is parameterized by three constants:
−CT : A transformation constant which bounds how far the transformed instance can move under one
source swap,
−CR: A fixed-source Lipschitz constant for the recovery map, and
−D: An additive source-swap term, measuring how much the recovery map itself can change when the
source instance changes but the transformed assignment is held fixed.
We record their effects in the following definition.
Definition 6.1 (Sensitivity-Preserving Reduction). Let I be a family of ordinary label cover instances or
left-predicate label cover instances on the same underlying graph and domain. Let T be a procedure that
transforms an instance I ∈I into another instance I′, and for each I ∈I let RI be a (possibly random-
ized) procedure that transforms an assignment π′ for I′ into an assignment π for I. For s, s′ ∈[0, 1] and
CT , CR, D ≥0, we say that (T , {RI}I∈I) is an (s, s′, CT , CR, D)-sensitivity-preserving reduction for I
if the following hold:
1. If I is satisfiable, then I′ = T (I) is also satisfiable.
2. If a (possibly random) assignment π′ for I′ satisfies E[valI′(π′)] ≥s′, then the assignment π =
RI(π′) satisfies E[valI(π)] ≥s.
3. The transformed instances T (I), for I ∈I, all live on one common underlying graph and common
alphabets, and for every source pair I, ˜I ∈I with SwapDist(I, ˜I) = 1 we have
SwapDist(T (I), T (˜I)) ≤CT .
4. For every fixed I ∈I and every two assignments π′, ˜π′ for T (I),
EMD(RI(π′), RI(˜π′)) ≤CR dH(π′, ˜π′).
5. For every source pair I, ˜I ∈I with SwapDist(I, ˜I) = 1 and every assignment π′ on the common
domain of T (I) and T (˜I),
EMD(RI(π′), R˜I(π′)) ≤D.
Lemma 6.2. Let I be a swap-closed family of instances on the same underlying graph and domain. Sup-
pose there exists an (s, s′, CT , CR, D)-sensitivity-preserving reduction (T , {RI}I∈I) for I. Write I′ =
T (I) := {T (I) : I ∈I}. Then every algorithm A′ on SwapClo(I′) that achieves soundness s′ on its
satisfiable members induces an algorithm A on I that achieves soundness s and satisfies
SwapSens(A, I) ≤CT CR SwapSens(A′, SwapClo(I′)) + D.

Proof. Let A′ be any algorithm on SwapClo(I′) that achieves soundness s′ on its satisfiable members.
Define an algorithm A on I as follows: on input I ∈I, let I′ := T (I), run A′ on I′ to obtain a (possibly
random) assignment π′ for I′, and output π ∼RI(π′).
If I is satisfiable, then Item 1 of Definition 6.1 ensures that I′ is satisfiable as well. Since I′ ∈I′ ⊆
SwapClo(I′), the assumption on A′ yields E[valI′(π′)] ≥s′, and Item 2 implies E[valI(π)] ≥s.
Let S := SwapSens(A′, SwapClo(I′)). Fix any source pair I, ˜I ∈I with SwapDist(I, ˜I) = 1. Write
I′ := T (I) and ˜I′ := T (˜I). By the same swap-path argument used in the proof of Lemma 3.2, Item 3
implies
EMD(A′(I′), A′(˜I′)) ≤CT S.
We decompose the perturbation as
EMD(A(I), A(˜I)) ≤EMD(RI(A′(I′)), RI(A′(˜I′))) + EMD(RI(A′(˜I′)), R˜I(A′(˜I′))).
Couple A′(I′) and A′(˜I′) optimally and apply Item 4 pointwise under that coupling to bound the first term
by
CR EMD(A′(I′), A′(˜I′)) ≤CT CRS.
For the second term, sample π′ ∼A′(˜I′) and apply Item 5 pointwise to obtain the bound D. Thus,
EMD(A(I), A(˜I)) ≤CT CRS + D.
Since the source one-swap pair (I, ˜I) was arbitrary, this proves
SwapSens(A, I) ≤CT CR SwapSens(A′, SwapClo(I′)) + D.
Minimizing the left-hand side over all algorithms A of this form and rearranging gives the final inequality.

Right-Degree Reduction in the Scalar Setting
We use the same right-degree reduction gadget as in Section 4. Concretely, we use the same transforma-
tion TDR, detailed in Algorithm 1, the same recovery map RDR, and the same fixed auxiliary choices of
expanders and neighbor orderings across a common family. Here we record only the scalar consequence of
that gadget for the sensitivity-reduction framework above.
Lemma 7.1. Let d ≥4 be even and λ ∈[0, 1). Let I be a swap-closed family of left-predicate label
cover instances on a common underlying graph and common alphabets, whose minimum right degree is at
least δV ≥d. For each right vertex v of this common underlying graph, let Dv denote its common degree
across the family, and fix a [Dv, d, λ]-expander Hv. Let I′ = SwapClo(TDR(I; d)). Then I′ consists of
right-regular left-predicate label cover instances with right degree d, and for any ε > 0, we have
SwapSensε+O(λ)(I′) ≥1
d SwapSensε(I).

8
Label Cover under Clause Swaps and Stable Recovery
For each CNF formula Φ, we construct a left-predicate label cover instance IΦ with low soundness and ex-
plicit control over how IΦ changes when clauses of the underlying CNF are swapped; this is the construction
used later to initialize the stage-0 family in Section 11. For the satisfiable CNF family used in that stage-0
initialization, we also prove a recovery theorem with perturbation guarantees, showing how to recover a
satisfying assignment of Φ from a labeling of IΦ with noticeable value. The first subsection constructs the
instances IΦ, and the second proves the recovery statement needed for stage 0.
8.1
Left-Predicate Label Cover under Clause Swaps
We begin with the left-predicate label cover, focusing on how the resulting label cover instance changes
under clause swaps in the original CNF. The obstacle is that the standard algebraic robust-PCP template
hardwires the clause predicate into one global low-degree polynomial. That is exactly what makes the
classical soundness argument work, but it is incompatible with locality: a single clause swap is highly local
in the source CNF’s table of clause indicators, but typically becomes global after low-degree extension,
ruining sensitivity.
31


## Page 35


Our construction separates the algebraic soundness task from the formula lookup task. The outer alge-
braic layer certifies only the low-degree structure of the assignment and witness polynomials, while a sep-
arate local Reed–Muller evaluation gadget supplies the relevant value of the source CNF’s clause-indicator
table at the distinguished point. This ensures that the formula-dependent part of the verifier stays inside the
left predicates, so local changes in the source formula appear as local predicate swaps while the underlying
graph and edge projections remain fixed. The result is a left-predicate label cover instance that serves as the
stage-0 family for the later construction.
The main result of the section is the following left-predicate label cover under clause swaps for suffi-
ciently large n.
Theorem 8.1 (Left-predicate label cover under clause swaps). Fix any constant c > 0. There exist n0 =
n0(c) and an absolute constant clc ≥1 such that the following holds for every integer n ≥n0. Let Φ
be any CNF formula on n variables, and let a := 4c + 7, m :=
log n
log log n, q := Θ((log n)a), and ε ≥
log−c n. Then there is an explicit reduction TRobust that maps Φ to a left-predicate label cover instance
IΦ = (U, V, E, ΣU, ΣV , {P Φ
u }u∈U, F = {fe}e∈E) with the following properties.
1. Completeness. If Φ is satisfiable, then val(IΦ) = 1.
2. Soundness. If Φ is unsatisfiable, then val(IΦ) ≤ε.
3. Change under clause swaps. If Φ and Φ′ differ by one clause swap, then IΦ and IΦ′ have the same
underlying graph, the same left and right alphabets, and the same edge projections, and only 2q8m+clc
left predicates change.
4. Parameters. Every instance IΦ satisfies
|V | ≤q3m+clc,
q11m−clc ≤|U| ≤q11m+clc,
|ΣV | = q3m+5,
|ΣU| ≤qqclc,
du ≤qclc for every u ∈U,
q8m+6 ≤dv ≤q8m+clc for every v ∈V,
We now begin the proof of this theorem; the construction of the label-cover instance proceeds in several
stages.
Preprocessing.
Fix a CNF formula Φ. Choose a field F of size q := |F| = Θ((log n)a) and a subset

Hm, where m = log n/ log log n.
An assignment a ∈{0, 1}n is now viewed as a function a : Hm →{0, 1}, and we let A : Fm →F
be its low-degree extension. Its degree is at most DA := m(|H| −1). Define the raw clause table BΦ :

below. Fix a common degree bound D := 8m|H|, which dominates all outer polynomials appearing below.

Objects and reference charts.
An object is a tuple Ω= (y, y′, z, z′) with y, y′, z, z′ ∈F3m+3 such that:
1. zi = z′
i for all i ∈[m], and
2. the six points z, y, y′, z′, ρ(z), ρ2(z) are affinely independent.
Let O denote the set of all objects. For Ω= (y, y′, z, z′) ∈O we write L(Ω) := aff(z, y, y′, z′, ρ(z), ρ2(z)),
which is now always a 5-dimensional affine subspace. For any function Q we denote by Q|L(Ω) the restric-
tion of Q’s domain to the points in L(Ω). We also fix the reference affine chart ψΩ: F5 →L(Ω) defined
by
ψΩ(0) = z,
ψΩ(e1) = y,
ψΩ(e2) = y′,
ψΩ(e3) = z′,
ψΩ(e4) = ρ(z),
ψΩ(e5) = ρ2(z),
where e1, . . . , e5 are the standard basis vectors of F5.
Equivalently, start from the product distribution in which z, y, y′ are uniform in F3m+3 and z′ is uniform
among points sharing the first m coordinates with z, and then condition on the affine-independence event in
item (2); this conditioned distribution is exactly the uniform distribution on O. There is an absolute constant
Cgeom ≥1 such that this conditioning changes each of the marginals of z, of y, and of (z, z′) by total-
variation distance at most Cgeom/q from the corresponding unconditioned uniform distribution on F3m+3 or
on the pairs sharing their first m coordinates. This is exactly the geometric regime used in the Dinur–Harsha
[20] object analysis.
Two standard ingredients enter next. The point-object theorem converts agreement among local object
restrictions into a short list of global low-degree candidates. The local evaluation theorem gives local
access to eBΦ(z) from the raw clause-indicator table while keeping the effect of clause swaps confined to a
controlled set of left predicates. We use both only as black boxes.
For two functions P, Q over the same domains, we denote by P ≡Q their point-wise equivalence.
An object oracle should be viewed as a table of local algebraic hypotheses: for each pair (Ω, τ) it returns
a k-tuple of degree-D polynomials on the 5-flat L(Ω). Different objects are not assumed to be globally
consistent a priori. The point-object theorem says that if, for many random triples (Ω, τ, x) with x ∈L(Ω),
this local tuple agrees with an ambient function f at the sampled point x, then except for a small error the
entire local tuple must already equal, on all of L(Ω), the restriction of one of a short list of global degree-D
tuples. The extra seed τ is only an external index for which local view is exposed; it does not change the
distribution of the sampled object Ωor of the sampled point x ∈L(Ω).
Theorem 8.3 (Point-object theorem with an external seed). There exist absolute constants cPO, CPO, Cerr ≥
1 such that the following holds. Let k ≥1, let T be any finite set, let f : F3m+3 →Fk be any function, and

Proof. This is the standard point-object theorem from Dinur–Harsha [20] and Harsha’s notes on the topic [32].
We simply fold the uniform seed τ ∈T into the oracle’s internal randomness. This does not change the
distribution of the sampled object Ωor of the sampled point x ∈L(Ω), so the same point-object analysis
applies. We introduce the extra seed only for the later local-evaluation gadget: there the left vertices will be
pairs (Ω, τ), with τ indexing the local decode/reject test attached to the distinguished point of Ω.
The next ingredient is a standard Reed–Muller decodable-reader gadget. We use it in a form specialized
to the raw clause-indicator table B: this is a convenient repackaging of the local decode/reject procedure
implicit in the Reed–Muller-based dPCP and zero-on-subcube constructions of Dinur–Harsha [20, Sec-
tion 6.1.2 and Appendix A], Harsha’s notes [32, Lecture 10, Section 10.2.3], and the closely related modern
presentation in [7, Section 7.3 and Appendix B.3].
Morally, this gadget supplies a locally checkable certificate that lets us recover the value eB(z) of the
low-degree extension from the raw table B without ever writing eB itself into the label-cover instance. The
auxiliary word Win(B) is that certificate, its coordinate index set Vin will later become the family of inner
right vertices, and for each pair (z, τ) the reader Evalz,τ chooses one local decoding/checking path for the
distinguished point z.
Given a table B : H3m+3 →F and a pair (z, τ), the local view of Evalz,τ consists of two ordered answer
strings: the raw-answer string, formed by the answers to its predetermined raw-table queries in B, and the
auxiliary-answer string, formed by the answers to its queries in Win(B).
Theorem 8.4 (Uniform local evaluation of the raw clause-indicator table). There exist an absolute constant
cin ≥1 and an explicit construction, depending only on (m, q, H), with the following properties.
1. It outputs a universal index set Vin for the coordinates of the auxiliary word with |Vin| = q3m+O(1).
2. It outputs a seed set T of size qO(1).
3. For each table B : H3m+3 →F it outputs an auxiliary word Win(B) ∈FVin.
4. For each point z ∈F3m+3 and seed τ ∈T, it specifies a local decode/reject test Evalz,τ that reads
at most Qin := qcin symbols from the auxiliary word Win(B) together with the values of B at a
predetermined set of at most Qin entries of the raw clause-indicator table, and outputs either reject or
a decoded value in F.
These tests satisfy:
−Completeness. Every local view of Evalz,τ induced by the pair
 Win(B), B

is accepted.
−Local correctness. Every accepted local view of Evalz,τ has a well-defined decoded value, and that
value is eB(z).
−Bounded locality. Every raw table point of H3m+3 participates in at most qcin tests Evalz,τ, and every
coordinate of Vin participates in at least one and at most qcin such tests.

In the label-cover instance, a left vertex u = (Ω, τ) will carry two kinds of information: the restriction
of the outer algebraic objects to the object Ω, and the auxiliary-answer string from one accepted local view
certifying the local evaluation at the distinguished point z of Ω. The inner edges simply project the slots of
that auxiliary-answer string to the corresponding coordinates of Vin, so the auxiliary coordinates are exactly
the right-vertex locations used to distribute this local certificate across the label-cover instance.
The label-cover encoding stores only auxiliary-answer strings, not the full local views. This is enough
because for fixed (z, τ) the raw-answer string is determined by the predetermined raw-table queries and will
be checked directly against BΦ inside the left predicate, while the coordinates in Vin will later serve as the
inner right vertices.
To keep the auxiliary-answer string length uniform, fix for each pair (z, τ) the ordered auxiliary-answer
string of Evalz,τ: the genuine auxiliary queries appear in their natural order, and if fewer than Qin such
queries are used, we pad by repeating auxiliary query positions. Thus every local view determines a padded
auxiliary-answer string in FQin, and the same auxiliary coordinate may occur in several transcript slots.

9
Alphabet Reduction
This section shrinks the right alphabet of the label cover instances obtained from the left-predicate label
cover under clause swaps in Section 8.1, while keeping enough locality to preserve the later sensitivity
lower bound. Throughout this section, we refer to the input label cover instance to the alphabet-reduction
step as the source instance. The main subtlety is that the recovery map for this step depends on the source
instance. To recover the label of a right vertex v in the source instance, we look at the labels suggested by
the neighboring left vertices through the maps f(u,v) on edges incident to v, and sample from the resulting
empirical distribution. Because this distribution depends both on the assignment to the reduced instance
and on the maps f(u,v) in the source instance, we must separately track perturbations of the assignment and
swaps of the source instance itself.
After recalling the required coding-theoretic facts, we define the transformation TAR and its instance-
specific recovery maps RAR,I, then we prove the resulting bounds on size, degrees, alphabets, soundness,
and the three locality quantities needed later in Section 11: transformation under source swaps, recovery
under assignment perturbations, and recovery under source swaps. If the source instance carries left predi-
cates in the sense of Definition 2.1, then the alphabet-reduction gadget simply transports them unchanged;
to avoid clutter we suppress them from the tuple notation.
9.1
The Alphabet Reduction Gadget
A mapping C : Σ →ˆΣk is called a code with minimum relative distance 1 −δ if for every a ̸= b ∈Σ the
strings C(a) and C(b) differ in at least (1 −δ)k coordinates.
Proposition 9.1 (Fact 5.5 of [20]). Suppose C : Σ →ˆΣk is a code with (relative) distance at least 1 −δ,
and let η > 2
√
δ. Then, for every word w ∈ˆΣk there are at most 2/η symbols a ∈Σ such that C(a) agrees
with w on at least an η fraction of the coordinates.
Proposition 9.2 (Remark 5.6 of [20]). For every 0 < δ < 1 and alphabet Σ, there exists a code C : Σ →ˆΣk
with relative distance 1 −δ where |ˆΣ| = O(δ−2) and k = O(δ−2 log |Σ|).
Later constructions will require a deterministic instantiation of such a code for a fixed source alphabet Σ
and a fixed distance parameter δ. To handle this, we will fix one such code once and for all. No algorithmic
explicitness beyond the existence statement above is needed for that use.
Suppose C : ΣV →ˆΣk
V is a code with (relative) distance 1−η3 for some η > 0 satisfying the guarantees
of Proposition 9.2 for δ = η3. We now describe our alphabet reduction transformation, which we denote
by TAR. The transformation maps label cover instances I = (U, V, E, ΣU, ΣV , F) to label cover instances
I′ = (U, V × [k], E′, ΣU, ˆΣV , F ′), where the set of edges E′ and the set of projections F ′ are defined
as follows: E′ = {(u, (v, i)) | (u, v) ∈E, i ∈[k]} and for each e = (u, (v, i)) ∈E′, the function
f′
e : ΣU →ˆΣV is defined as f′
e(a) = C(f(u,v)(a))i.

Lemma 9.3. Let I be a swap-closed family of label cover instances I = (U, V, E, ΣU, ΣV , F) with average
left degree ¯dU, average right degree ¯dV , maximum left degree ∆U, and minimum right degree δV , and let
I′ = TAR(I). Then for any ε > 0 and η ∈(0, 1/4), the right alphabet of instances in I′ is ˆΣV and satisfies
|ˆΣV | = O(η−6). Moreover, writing
sAR(ε, η) :=
ε
√η + 2√η
and
k := O(η−6 log |ΣV |),
the following hold.
1. If I ∈I is satisfiable, then TAR(I) is satisfiable.

The point of Lemma 9.3 is that the alphabet-reduction step yields both a bound for how the recovery
changes under assignment perturbations and a separate bound for how the recovery changes under source
swaps. Because RAR,I depends on I through the distributions pv, these two bounds are not interchangeable:
the first only controls perturbations of assignments on one transformed instance, whereas Item 5 controls
what happens when the source instance itself changes.
9.2
Reduced Label Cover
In this section, we summarize the combined effect of the applying alphabet reduction and then degree
reduction on a label cover instance. For the soundness thresholds recorded below, we henceforth assume
0 < ε < 1/16, so that 3√ε and 4√ε both lie in (0, 1). Let I = (U, V, E, ΣU, ΣV , F) be a label cover
instance with |U| = n, |V | = m, average left degree ¯dU, average right degree ¯dV , maximum right degree
∆V , maximum left degree ∆U, and minimum right degree δV . Table 2 tracks how these parameters change
when we first apply the alphabet-reduction map induced by a code C : ΣV →σk of relative distance 1 −η3,
and then run the degree-reduction step based on [Dv, d, λ]-expanders tailored to the right-degree Dv of each
vertex v ∈V .
We instantiate C using Lemma 9.3 (alphabet reduction) with η = ε/4 (which in turn appeals to Propo-
sition 9.2), yielding a right alphabet σ of size Θ(ε−6) and block length k = Θ(ε−6 log |ΣV |), while the
alphabet-reduction soundness threshold becomes 3√ε. For the degree reduction, we take λ = √ε, choose
d = Θ(ε−1), and for each right-degree value D that occurs in the common source graph we fix once and

10
Decodable PCPs and Composition
This section develops the decodable PCP ingredient used in the low-soundness label cover construction and
proves the composition theorem that is applied at each stage of the iteration in Section 11. Starting from a
52


## Page 56


suitable outer label cover instance and a decodable PCP (dPCP) decoder, we construct the next label cover
instance in the iteration and record the completeness, soundness, and stability properties needed there. In
this section, the “source” instance is the outer label cover instance fed into the composition, so “source
swaps” mean swaps on that outer instance. We state the result for left-predicate label cover instances so
that the same theorem applies before and after swaps. In the special case of ordinary label cover, where all
predicates are identically 1, the argument is identical.
10.1
Decodable PCPs
For a finite alphabet Σ, we let CircuitSATΣ denote the satisfiability problem for circuits whose inputs range
over Σ: given a circuit C : Σk →{0, 1}, the instance is a Yes instance if there exists y ∈Σk with C(y) = 1
and a No instance otherwise. Any such vector y is called a satisfying assignment for C. Throughout this
section, the decision complexity of a circuit means the size of its given circuit representation. In particular,
we do not minimize over equivalent circuits, and we use the same size measure for the local decoding maps
and admissible-neighborhood circuits below.
Informally, a PCP decoder is given a circuit C, an index j ∈[k], and oracle access to an auxiliary proof
string π ∈σm(n), where n denotes the decision complexity of C. The proof is simply an additional string
over the proof alphabet σ; it is not part of the input circuit, and for our purposes we only need the existence
of such a string in the completeness case. Using r(n) random coins, the decoder chooses q(n) locations of
π to query and then applies a local decoding rule of complexity s(n) to the queried symbols, with the goal
of recovering the target symbol yj. Thus m(n) is the proof length, while q(n), r(n), and s(n) are the query
complexity, randomness complexity, and local-decoder complexity, respectively.
Definition 10.1 (PCP decoder). Fix a proof alphabet σ. A PCP decoder for CircuitSATΣ is a probabilistic
polynomial-time algorithm D that, on input a circuit C : Σk →{0, 1} whose decision complexity is n and
an index j ∈[k], uses r(n) random coins and outputs:
1. A tuple of query locations I = (i1, . . . , iq) ∈[m(n)]q for some q = q(n) and proof length m(n), and
2. A deterministic local decoding map f : σq →Σ ∪{⊥} whose decision complexity is at most s(n).
After reading the proof symbols at positions I, the decoder returns f(πI), where π ∈σm(n) is the purported
proof and πI := (πi1, . . . , πiq) is the queried substring.
Definition 10.2 (Decodable PCP). Let δ : Z>0 →[0, 1] and L : Z>0 →Z>0. A PCP decoder D for
CircuitSATΣ is a decodable PCP (dPCP) with soundness error δ and list size L if the following conditions
hold for every circuit C : Σk →{0, 1}.
−Completeness: For every satisfying assignment y ∈Σk there exists a proof π ∈σm(n) such that,
when j ∈[k] is chosen uniformly at random and the randomness of D is taken over (I, f), we have
Prj,I,f



satisfying assignments for C with ℓ≤L(n) such that

Lemma 10.3 (Theorem 6.5 of [20]). There exist constants a1, a2, α, γ > 0 such that for every δ ≥n−α and
input alphabet Σ of size at most nγ, CircuitSATΣ has a dPCP decoder with soundness error δ and list size
L ≤2/δ, query complexity n1/8, proof alphabet size at most nγ, proof length at most na1, local-decoder
decision complexity at most na1, and randomness complexity a2 log n.
10.2
Composition
We next adapt the composition framework of Dinur and Harsha [20] to left-predicate label cover instances.
The key point is that the inner decoder should see only the set of admissible tuples of neighboring right
labels at each outer left vertex, rather than the full left alphabet ΣU. This is captured by the following notion
of local description complexity.
Definition 10.4 (Admissible Neighborhood Tuples and Local Description Complexity). Let
I =
 U, V, E, ΣU, ΣV , {Pu}u∈U, F = {fe}e∈E

be a left-predicate label cover instance. For every u ∈U, fix an ordering
NI(u) = (v1, . . . , vdegI(u))
of the neighbors of u. A tuple (b1, . . . , bdegI(u)) ∈ΣdegI(u)
V
is admissible at u if there exists a ∈ΣU such
that Pu(a) = 1 and f(u,vt)(a) = bt for every t ∈[degI(u)]. The admissible neighborhood set at u is the set
of all admissible tuples, denoted by
NI(u) ⊆ΣdegI(u)
V
.
We say that I has local description complexity at most S if, for every u ∈U, there exists a circuit Cu :
ΣdegI(u)
V
→{0, 1} of decision complexity at most S such that C−1
u (1) = NI(u). A family has local
description complexity at most S if every instance in the family does.
At a high level, the composition procedure proceeds as follows; an illustration is given in Figure 4 and
a dPCP decoder D. Fix an outer instance I = (U, V, E, ΣU, ΣV , {Pu}u∈U, F). For each outer right vertex
v ∈V and decoder randomness string r ∈{0, 1}r, the composition creates one composed left vertex (v, r),
and for each outer left vertex u ∈U and proof position t ∈[m], it creates one composed right vertex (u, t).
A label of (v, r) is a dV × m matrix, where dV is the right-degree of V , over the proof alphabet σ: the
columns are proof positions, and the rows index the at most dV neighbors of v. If u1, . . . , ut(v) are the
actual neighbors of v in the outer instance, where t(v) := degI(v), then the rows of this matrix which are
indexed by u1, . . . , ut(v) are the active rows, and the remaining rows are unused rows, which are added only
so that every assignment has the same size. For every active row i and every proof position t ∈[m], we
include an edge from (v, r) to (ui, t), and a projection constraint on this edge. To define the left-predicate
for (v, t), the decoder D is run on (Cui, ji) with randomness r, where C−1
ui (1) = NI(ui) and v is the ji-th
neighbor of ui. The output selects a few columns ti,1, . . . , ti,q from each active row of the matrix and uses
these to define the left predicate for (v, r). This means that the query pattern affects the left predicates, but
not the edges of the resulting label cover instance.

The explicit construction is spelled out in Algorithm 2, and is shown pictorially for a vertex v in Figure 4.
For a fixed outer underlying graph, the vertex and edge sets of the label cover instance obtained by applying
composition depend only on that graph and on the decoder parameters, not on the query tuples. Instance by
instance, the only part of the composed verifier that changes is the family of left predicates. Equivalently,
this is a composition on a common padded graph: as in the overview above, the label given to each left vertex
is a matrix where active rows are the rows corresponding to actual neighbors, unused rows are padding, and
the coordinate-projection edges are fixed in advance across all instances in the family.
The following theorem provides the guarantees that we obtain from composition.

59


## Page 63


Local description complexity.
Fix a composed left vertex (v, r) and let t(v) = degI(v). A tuple of
symbols on the active rows of (v, r), namely the rows indexed by the actual neighbors u1, . . . , ut(v) of v
in Algorithm 2, extends to an admissible left label in the composed instance if and only if the decoder
outputs on the queried coordinates of those rows all agree on the same non-⊥symbol. Thus the admissible
neighborhood set at (v, r) is recognized by a circuit that evaluates at most t(v) ≤dV local maps gi,r, each on
coordinates among the m positions of one row, and checks equality of their outputs. Its decision complexity
is therefore poly(dV , m, s(N)).
The remaining structural bounds follow directly from Algorithm 2: the left side is V × {0, 1}r, the right
side is U × [m], each composed left vertex (v, r) has exactly t(v)m = degI(v)m ≤dV m neighbors, and
each composed right vertex (u, t) has exactly degI(u)2r ≤dU2r neighbors. In particular, if I has no isolated
vertices on either side, then neither does I ⊛D. This proves item (6).

A source swap means one swap on the input side of the reduction currently under discussion: globally
this is a swap in ΦNbase, while inside one stage it may mean a swap in the preceding label cover family. The
stage families inherited from Section 8 still carry left predicates, so we keep that language throughout the
iteration to maintain a common underlying graph under swaps; in the ordinary label cover special case, all
predicates are identically 1.
11.1
How the Constants Evolve
Theorem 10.5 is the concrete one-layer composition statement. The next lemmas extract from it the update
rules that are used later: how the transformation changes under source swaps, how the recovery changes
under assignment perturbations, and how the recovery changes under source swaps propagate through one
more fixed layer. In these lemmas, the input side of the current reduction step is the source family and the
image side is the target family; we reserve “output” for assignments or distributions produced by algorithms
or recovery maps. We begin with the following immediate specialization of Lemma 2.3: once a swap-closed
family lives on a common underlying graph, a one-swap bound for the recovery drift extends to arbitrary
swap distance by the same shortest-path triangle-inequality argument.
Lemma 11.1 (Pathwise extension of a one-swap recovery bound). Let J be a swap-closed family of in-

The Fixed Alphabet/Degree Reduction on a Common Family
The following definitions and lemmas describe how red◦
ε—alphabet-then-degree reduction from Lemma 9.4
—is used in a single stage update. Later stage statements will also require every left and right vertex to

Under this encoding, each finite-alphabet gate appearing in Cu can be replaced by a Boolean subcircuit

of size poly(S, t, |ΣV |) = poly(S, ∆U, |ΣV |). In I(1), the neighbors of u are the blocks
(v1, 1), . . . , (v1, k), . . . , (vt, 1), . . . , (vt, k).
A tuple of block labels extends across u in I(1) if and only if two conditions hold. First, for every r ∈[t],
