Degree reduction.
If the proof of Lemma 9.4 stops after alphabet reduction, there is nothing more to
prove. Otherwise, let I(2) = TDR(I(1); d) be the degree-reduced instance, with d = Θ(ε−1). The left
vertices are unchanged. Each original right vertex v is replaced by a cloud of vertices (v, i), and every orig-
inal edge incident to u is replaced by several edges whose projections are exact copies of the corresponding
original projection. Therefore, a tuple of labels on the new neighborhood of u extends across u in I(2) if
and only if the labels on all copies belonging to the same original neighbor v are equal, and the resulting
collapsed tuple belongs to the admissible neighborhood set of u in I(1). A circuit can check this by per-
forming equality tests inside each cloud-copy group and then evaluating the circuit for u in I(1). Its size is
poly(S, ε−1, ∆U, ∆V , |ΣV |).
This proves the stated bound for red◦
ε(I), uniformly over all I ∈I.
In Section 11, every application of Lemma 11.8 has input right alphabet size |ΣV | ≤ε−O(1) at the rele-
vant stage, so the polynomial dependence on |ΣV | still yields local description complexity ε−O(1) throughout
the iteration.
Finally, we observe that applying alphabet-then-degree reduction preserves the property of not having
isolated vertices. We record this because the stage invariants below require every left and right vertex to
have degree at least 1. Together with the analogous preservation fact for composition from Theorem 10.5,
this lets us carry non-isolatedness through each stage of the iteration, until the final exact-size padding step
where isolated left vertices are added deliberately. To see that this is the case, observe that in both branches
of red◦
ε, the left vertices are unchanged, and each new right vertex is a copy of an old right vertex attached
to copies of its incident edges. Consequently, if the input instance has no isolated vertices on either side,
then neither does its reduced image. We record this because the stage invariants below require every left
and right vertex to have degree at least 1. Together with the analogous preservation fact for composition
from Theorem 10.5, this lets us carry non-isolatedness through each stage of the iteration, until the final
exact-size padding step where isolated left vertices are added deliberately.
11.3
Initialization of the Left-Predicate Label Cover under Clause Swaps
We initialize the stage-0 reduction data from the left-predicate label cover under clause swaps supplied by
Section 8.1. The point of this subsection is not merely to produce one family I0, but to put the proof into the
exact form needed to start the iterative update from Proposition 11.12: we need a satisfiable stage-0 family
on a common target graph and common alphabets, with local description complexity, degrees, alphabets,
and the three constants controlling swaps and perturbations already polynomial in 1/ε0, together with a
direct recovery that turns value at least 9√ε0 on the target side into source value at least 1 −ηFY.

composition. We then compose once to obtain the actual stage-0 family I0. Finally, we build the stage-0

ments into a threshold selector. Unlike the later stages, the initial family G0—obtained by running the robust
PCP transformation from Section 8 on the initial family of CNF formulas—still has very large right degree,

Part III
From Label Cover to Covering Problems
12
Set Cover
This section proves the two main set-cover sensitivity lower bounds used later in the locality argument, one
via the IKW route and one via the Dinur–Harsha route. The former gives a lower bound for logarithmic-
approximation algorithms, while the latter yields a polynomial sensitivity lower bound. In both cases, the
hard instances have bounded set size and frequency. Both start from the same Feige–Lund–Yannakakis re-
duction from label cover to set cover, so we first isolate the common transformation and recovery statements.
In the set cover problem, an instance is a pair (U, F = (S1, . . . , Sm)), where U is a finite ground set and
F is an indexed family of subsets of U. The goal is to find a subfamily S ⊆[m] of minimum size whose
union covers U, i.e., S
i∈S Si = U. We call an instance feasible if some subfamily covers U, equivalently if
Sm
i=1 Si = U.
For two instances I = (U, F = (S1, . . . , Sm)) and ˜I = (U, ˜F = ( ˜S1, . . . , ˜Sm)) over the same
ground set and indexed family, we define the swap distance by SwapDist(I, ˜I) = Pm
i=1 |Si△˜Si|. That is,
SwapDist(I, ˜I) counts the number of single-element membership changes across all sets (equivalently, the
Hamming distance between the two incidence matrices). We identify a subfamily S ⊆[m] with its indicator
vector, and define the Hamming distance by Ham(S, ˜S) := |S△˜S|. For a (possibly randomized) algorithm
A and an instance I, define the swap sensitivity by SwapSens(A, I) := max˜I
EMD(A(I),A(˜I))
SwapDist(I,˜I) , where the
maximum ranges over instances ˜I on the same ground set and index set with SwapDist(I, ˜I) > 0, and the
earth mover’s distance uses Ham as the underlying metric on outputs. For a family I, set SwapSens(A, I) :=
maxI∈I SwapSens(A, I). For α ≥1, let SwapSensα(I) be the minimum SwapSens(A, I) over all algo-
rithms A defined on all set cover instances and satisfying the α-approximation guarantee on every feasible
I ∈I.
84


## Page 88


Parameter guide.
In the reduction proofs below, nLC denotes the size parameter of the source label cover
instance, NSC the size of the set-cover universe, and mSC := |F| the number of sets. For the IKW route,
nLC refers to the total number of label-cover vertices; for the Dinur–Harsha route below, we later normalize
it to the left-side size. Bare n is reserved for theorem statements.
12.1
Reduction from Label Cover to Set Cover
We follow the basic reduction from label cover to set cover [27] based on the (m, l)-set system [27, 47],
defined below. An (m, l)-set system consists of a universe B and collection of subsets C = {C1, . . . , Cm}
such that, if a collection of at most l sets in {C1, . . . , Cm, ¯C1, . . . , ¯Cm} covers B, then the collection must
contain both Ci and ¯Ci for some i.
Lemma 12.1 ([47]). An (m, l)-set system with a universe size |B| = O(22lm2) exists, and can be con-
structed in 2O(l)mO(1) time.
Fix m and l, and let (B, {C1, . . . , Cm}) be an (m, l)-set system. We define a transformation TSC that
maps a label cover instance I = (U, V, E, ΣU, ΣV , P, F), where P = {Pu}u∈U and |ΣV | = m, to a set
cover instance I′ = (E × B, F). Let
ΛI := (V × ΣV ) ⊔(U × ΣU).
We create the indexed family F = (Sλ)λ∈ΛI of subsets of E ×B, writing Sv,x for S(v,x) and Su,y for S(u,y).
When the source instance carries left predicates, we encode them directly in the left-label sets below so that
inadmissible left labels become useless for covering. After the reduction to set cover, that distinction has
been absorbed into the covering instance itself.
−For every vertex v ∈V and label x ∈ΣV , set Sv,x = S
e∋v{e} × Cx.
−For every vertex u ∈U and label y ∈ΣU, set
Su,y =
(S
e∋u{e} × Cfe(y)
if Pu(y) = 1,
∅
if Pu(y) = 0.
Repeated subsets with different indices remain distinct members of the indexed family F. If ∆(I) denotes
the maximum degree of the label cover instance, then every set in F contains at most ∆(I) · |B| elements,
and each element of E × B is contained in at most |ΣU| + |ΣV | many indexed sets.
Next, we describe the recovery map RSC,I, which depends on the source instance I. Given a subfamily
S ⊆ΛI, define label sets Lu = {y ∈ΣU : (u, y) ∈S and Pu(y) = 1} for each u ∈U, and Lv = {x ∈
ΣV : (v, x) ∈S} for each v ∈V . Intuitively, Lu (resp. Lv) records the labels that the set cover solution
assigns to u (resp. v). To obtain a randomized assignment π, we independently pick π(u) uniformly from
Lu and π(v) uniformly from Lv for every u ∈U and v ∈V (if some Lu or Lv is empty we choose an
arbitrary fixed label). When the source instance is fixed, we may suppress the subscript I.
Lemma 12.2. Let I = (U, V, E, ΣU, ΣV , P, F) be a label cover instance, and let I′ = TSC(I). Then the
following hold.
−If I is satisfiable, then I′ has a cover of size at most |U| + |V |.
−Let S ⊆ΛI be any cover of I′. After deleting from S all selected indices whose associated sets are
empty, the cover property is unchanged and the size does not increase. For a fixed edge e = (u, v),
the nonempty selected sets on the slice {e} × B ⊂E × B are exactly the sets
Cx
with x ∈Lv
and
Cfe(y)
with y ∈Lu.

Proof. If I is satisfiable, choose a satisfying label for each u ∈U and v ∈V . The corresponding family

fe(y) = x, so Cx ∪Cfe(y) = B.
Let S ⊆ΛI be any cover of I′, and delete from it all selected indices whose associated sets are empty
to obtain S′. The set covered by S′ is unchanged and |S′| ≤|S|. Fix an edge e = (u, v). On the slice
{e} × B, a selected right-label set contributes exactly Cx for some x ∈Lv, while a selected left-label set
contributes either Cfe(y) for some admissible y ∈Lu or nothing at all. After the empty indices are removed,

For fixed I, toggling one selected index changes only one label set Lu or Lv. Hence it affects at most
one output coordinate. Coupling the uniform choices identically on all unchanged coordinates gives
EMD(RSC,I(S), RSC,I( ˜S)) ≤Ham(S, ˜S).
If I and ˜I differ only by a predicate swap at u0, then the admissibility filter changes only the label set
at u0: all Lv are identical and all Lu with u ̸= u0 are identical. Hence the recovered assignments can differ
only at coordinate u0, which gives
EMD(RSC,I(S), RSC,˜I(S)) ≤1.
For a projection swap the predicates Pu do not change, so the recovery map itself is unchanged.

13
Dominating Set
This section proves the two main dominating-set sensitivity lower bounds: the IKW-based bound Theo-
rem 13.3 and the Dinur–Harsha-based bound Theorem 13.4. We first isolate the common reduction from set
cover to dominating set and then specialize it in the two approximation regimes.
Parameter guide.
In the lower-bound proofs below, NSC denotes the size of the source set-cover universe,
mSC := |F| the number of sets, nDS the target number of vertices, and Γ the parameter bounding both set
size and element frequency in the dominating-set reduction. Bare n is reserved for theorem statements.
13.1
Reduction from Set Cover to Dominating Set
Fix a parameter Γ ≥1. We begin with the classical reduction from set cover to dominating set due to [11];
we refer to it as TDS. Given a set cover instance I = (U = {u1, . . . , uNSC}, F = (S1, . . . , SmSC))
whose maximum set size and maximum element frequency are at most Γ, form the bipartite incidence graph
G0 = (U ∪F, E0) with E0 = {(u, S) | u ∈S}. Introduce an additional set
W = {w1, . . . , w⌈mSC/Γ⌉}
100


## Page 104


of helper vertices and connect each set vertex Si to the helper vertex w⌈i/Γ⌉. Then every helper vertex has
degree at most Γ, and the choice of helper edges is deterministic once the index set [mSC] and the parameter
Γ are fixed. Let G = TDS(I) denote the resulting graph. It is bipartite and satisfies
∆(G) ≤Γ + 1,
|V (G)| = NSC + mSC +
lmSC
Γ
m
.
Let sc(I) be the optimum set cover size of I, and let ds(G) be the optimum dominating set size of
G. For approximation guarantees we only apply this reduction to feasible set-cover instances, and the hard
instances from Section 12 are feasible. For the later sensitivity pullback, however, it is convenient to keep the
recovery map defined on arbitrary neighboring instances as well. For every set-cover instance I = (U, F)
and every element u ∈U, define
σI(u) ∈[mSC] ∪{⊥}
by a fixed deterministic rule: if u belongs to at least one set, let σI(u) be the smallest index i with u ∈Si;
otherwise set σI(u) := ⊥. When I is feasible, σI(u) ∈[mSC] for every u ∈U. Define the recovery map
RDS,I on vertex subsets D ⊆V (G) by
RDS,I(D) := {i ∈[mSC] : Si ∈D} ∪{σI(u) : u ∈D ∩U, σI(u) ̸= ⊥}.
In words, we keep all selected set-vertices, add one fixed incident set for each selected element-vertex when
such a set exists, and ignore helper vertices.
Lemma 13.1. Let I = (U, F) be a set-cover instance and let G = TDS(I). Then the following hold.
−If I is feasible and D is a dominating set of G, then RDS,I(D) is a set cover of I.
−For every vertex subset D ⊆V (G), |RDS,I(D)| ≤|D|.
−Define ϕI(Si) = i, ϕI(u) = σI(u), and ϕI(w) = ⊥. Then RDS,I(D) = ϕI(D)\{⊥}. Consequently,
for any two vertex subsets D, ˜D ⊆V (G),
RDS,I(D)△RDS,I( ˜D) ⊆ϕI(D△˜D),
and therefore |RDS,I(D)△RDS,I( ˜D)| ≤|D△˜D|.
−If I and ˜I differ by toggling exactly one membership relation (u0, Sj), and the designated choices
σI, σ˜I are defined by the same deterministic rule, then for any fixed vertex subset D we have
|RDS,I(D)△RDS,˜I(D)| ≤2.
Proof. Assume first that I is feasible, let D be a dominating set of G, and fix any element u ∈U. If u ∈D,
then the set index σI(u) belongs to RDS,I(D) and covers u. If u /∈D, then because D dominates u and
only set-vertices are adjacent to u, some selected set-vertex Si ∈D contains u, so i ∈RDS,I(D) and u is
covered. Thus RDS,I(D) is a set cover.
Every selected set-vertex contributes at most one set index, every selected element-vertex contributes
at most one set index, and helper vertices contribute nothing. Duplicates only decrease the image size, so
|RDS,I(D)| ≤|D|.
The identity RDS,I(D) = ϕI(D) \ {⊥} is immediate from the definition of ϕI. Now fix another vertex
subset ˜D ⊆V (G). Therefore
RDS,I(D)△RDS,I( ˜D) ⊆ϕI(D△˜D),
which implies
|RDS,I(D)△RDS,I( ˜D)| ≤|D△˜D|.
101


## Page 105


If I and ˜I differ by toggling one membership relation (u0, Sj), then the deterministic rule can change
only the designated choice for u0; all other values of ϕI and ϕ˜I agree. Hence, for a fixed D, the image of D
can change only through the contribution of u0, and at worst one set index is inserted, deleted, or replaced
by another. Therefore |RDS,I(D)△RDS,˜I(D)| ≤2.
In particular, for every feasible set-cover instance I, sc(I) ≤ds(G) ≤sc(I) + |W| ≤sc(I) + |F|
Γ + 1.
The lower bound follows by applying RDS,I to an optimal dominating set, while the upper bound is obtained
from an optimal set cover by selecting the corresponding set-vertices and all helper vertices.
For later exact-size padding, we also fix the following gadget. For integers ∆≥2 and t ≥0, let P∆,t
denote any fixed star forest on t vertices whose component sizes are all at most ∆and whose dominating
number is exactly ⌈t/∆⌉; for example, write t = a∆+ b with 0 ≤b < ∆, take a copies of K1,∆−1, and if
b > 0 add one copy of K1,b−1 (interpreting K1,0 as an isolated vertex).

tI := nDS −|V (G)|,

vertex subsets D ⊆V (HI) by
